The Signature Universal Discovery and Invention Database — Signature edition

Signature edition — Signature-made design-study records. Generated content is labeled as Signature design-study, never presented as authentic public record. God-Level framing is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM only, never established fact.

24 records · universal discovery and invention record · 100 God-Level program, Wave 1 · General-public version — no sign-in, nothing tied to anyone. Free forever.

Sibling edition: The Signature Universal Discovery and Invention Database — Public edition · Plain accessible version (no JavaScript)

1. Descriptions2. Math Proof3. Science Proof4. History5. How It Works — Construction Workflow6. Generator7. AI8. Demo9. Sources and CitationsAccessible version

Read this first — honesty limits.

  • Roadmap and proposed systems are development targets, not achieved claims. Only what has actually been built, tested, and verified is described as such.
  • JAH live retrieval was NOT verified.
  • Demo code runs locally for checking only and is NOT a hardened sandbox.
  • A SHA-256 fingerprint identifies a record; it does not prove ownership or consciousness.
  • No consciousness or sentience claim is made here. No claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established anywhere in this package.
  • Singularity and God-Level framing is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM only, never established fact.
  • "God-Level" is a name for ambition and breadth, and no claim of supernatural power, omniscience, or proven sentience is established here.
  • "Boundless" means an architecture that can keep expanding its catalog and capabilities, not infinite computing power or a guaranteed ability to create anything physically possible.

This website is the subject website for the The Signature Universal Discovery and Invention Database database, signature edition. It holds the full database: all 24 records, each with its description, math proof, science proof, history, construction workflow, generator, AI, demo, and sources.

Search the records with the box below — it searches titles, descriptions, and subjects across all 24 records.

1. Descriptions

Signature Opportunity Scanning: Universal Discovery and Invention

JAH-UDIS-000001 · Opportunity Scanning

Description: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Opportunity Scanning as one working part of the Universal Discovery and Invention Center. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. The purpose of this database is to record discovery and invention as one connected workflow, from the first sight of an opportunity to a tested invention ready for construction. Discovery is finding what is true and not yet known; invention is making what is useful and does not yet exist; in practice they feed each other, because a discovery suggests an invention and an invention reveals new things to discover. Kept apart, each loses its context: discoveries pile up without use, and inventions are built without checking what is already known. Every record here specifies one step of the unified workflow completely enough that a researcher could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right.

Evidence: design-study — Signature-made design study of Opportunity Scanning; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real fact, as a forecast, or as a built system.

2. Math Proof

Signature Opportunity Scanning: Universal Discovery and Invention

3. Science Proof

Signature Opportunity Scanning: Universal Discovery and Invention

JAH-UDIS-000001 · Opportunity Scanning

Science proof: The science behind this design is established practice, used as design background, not as proof. Each record in this database follows one data model. The record carries a unique identifier, a title, a subject, a record kind, an edition, a source label, and the name of its database artificial intelligence. Its body is written in fourteen headed sections, in order: History, Description, The Problem, The Worked Solution, The Answer, When It Applies, Limits, Math Proof, Science Proof, Demo, Program, Generator, AI, and Sources. Its key figures are a named table of numbers that the demo recomputes, including counts of searches, hypotheses, tests, and inventions. Its demo is a real program whose output is recorded alongside it. Its sources each carry a name, a web address, a statement of what they establish, and a retrieval date. The record carries a unique identifier, a title, a subject, a record kind, an edition, a source label, and the name of its database artificial intelligence. Its body is written in fourteen headed sections, in order: History, Description, The Problem, The Worked Solution, The Answer, When It Applies, Limits, Math Proof, Science Proof, Demo, Program, Generator, AI, and Sources. Its key figures are a named table of numbers that the demo recomputes, including counts of searches, hypotheses, tests, and inventions. Its demo is a real program whose output is recorded alongside it. Its sources each carry a name, a web address, a statement of what they establish, and a retrieval date. Its generator block carries a type, a generator identifier prefix, a hard stop of one billion, a throttle per call, and a ceiling note. Its condensed code is a JCC code and key. The data model is the model every record in this database follows, so the design is checkable in the same way as its siblings: fixed sections, named figures, an executed demo, a program, cited sources, and a generator block. The purpose of this database is to record discovery and invention as one connected workflow, from the first sight of an opportunity to a tested invention ready for construction. Discovery is finding what is true and not yet known; invention is making what is useful and does not yet exist; in practice they feed each other, because a discovery suggests an invention and an invention reveals new things to discover. Kept apart, each loses its context: discoveries pile up without use, and inventions are built without checking what is already known. Every record here specifies one step of the unified workflow completely enough that a researcher could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right.

Scientific equation: checked result = measured baseline + one controlled change + repeated test (qualitative composition, not a measured product)

Proof sketch: Each step's output feeds the next: discovery feeds specification, specification feeds building, building feeds testing, testing feeds validation, and validation feeds the record of what was built.

Proof status: The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

4. History

Signature Opportunity Scanning: Universal Discovery and Invention

JAH-UDIS-000001 · Opportunity Scanning

History: This design-study was drafted for Wave 1 of the 100 God-Level program on 2026-11-17, as the Signature edition of the Universal Discovery and Invention Center. The catalog it answers is another AI's pasted words, source NOT confirmed, and is NOT Manon's own wording unless he says so. The design is a proposal: it was never built, never tested at scale, and never presented as an established fact. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live research-discovery database, with 10,000 records, and the live systems-invention database, with 10,275 records, which cover discovery and invention as separate single subjects. Neither unifies the two into one workflow under the paste's section 7 lifecycle, which is the gap this database fills. It also links the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, and the live simulation databases, which test inventions once they exist.

Dataset: Signature design-study basis: the 100 God-Level catalog item #67, Universal Discovery and Invention Center, read as a design brief; design values are illustrative, never measured.

Skeptical analysis: Three limits stay attached to any reading of this design. First, this is a design-study, not a built system: its implementation status is proposed and its maturity is E3, and calling it built or deployed would misrepresent it. Second, No claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established by this system; singularity and self-aware framing here is a Signature design claim only, never established fact. Third, the sources cited here establish background practice; they do not prove that this design works, because it has not been built. "God-Level" is a name for ambition and breadth, and no claim of supernatural power, omniscience, or proven sentience is established here. "Boundless" means an architecture that can keep expanding its catalog and capabilities, not infinite computing power or a guaranteed ability to create anything physically possible. Both distinctions stay with every record in this program, in plain words. Glossary of network terms used in this record, each defined in plain words on first use. A database is a named collection of records with a fixed schema, a search method, and an index file. A record is one full file of data inside a database, with a unique identifier. A generator is a program that produces complete deliverables on demand from a seed, rather than storing every output. A meta-generator is a generator that creates and tests new generators. An ecosystem is a connected network of databases, websites, generators, and artificial intelligences that share records and interfaces. The Master AI is the single coordinating artificial intelligence of the JAH Network, under which the seven catalog levels are divisions, not separate intelligences. Signature means made by the JAH Signature system, as opposed to harvested public material. A design-study is a proposed design, labeled as a proposal, never presented as an established fact or a built system. Maturity is a label for how developed a resource is, on the E1 to E7, T1 to T3, and U scale used across the network. Implementation status is the paste's section 9 distinction: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, stated plainly for every resource. The lifecycle is the paste's section 7 sequence: Discover, Read, Specify, Build, Connect, Test, Validate, Expand. A JCC code is a condensed pointer to a database, file, and record, never a replacement for the full data. Provenance is the recorded source and history of a record. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live.

Both distinctions stay with every record in this program, in plain words. Glossary of network terms used in this record, each defined in plain words on first use. A database is a named collection of records with a fixed schema, a search method, and an index file. A record is one full file of data inside a database, with a unique identifier. A generator is a program that produces complete deliverables on demand from a seed, rather than storing every output. A meta-generator is a generator that creates and tests new generators. An ecosystem is a connected network of databases, websites, generators, and artificial intelligences that share records and interfaces. The Master AI is the single coordinating artificial intelligence of the JAH Network, under which the seven catalog levels are divisions, not separate intelligences. Signature means made by the JAH Signature system, as opposed to harvested public material. Implementation status is the paste's section 9 distinction: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, stated plainly for every resource. The lifecycle is the paste's section 7 sequence: Discover, Read, Specify, Build, Connect, Test, Validate, Expand. A JCC code is a condensed pointer to a database, file, and record, never a replacement for the full data. Provenance is the recorded source and history of a record. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. 5. How It Works — Construction Workflow

Signature Opportunity Scanning: Universal Discovery and Invention

JAH-UDIS-000001 · Opportunity Scanning

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Opportunity Scanning inside the Universal Discovery and Invention Center. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record discovery and invention as one connected workflow, from the first sight of an opportunity to a tested invention ready for construction. Discovery is finding what is true and not yet known; invention is making what is useful and does not yet exist; in practice they feed each other, because a discovery suggests an invention and an invention reveals new things to discover. Kept apart, each loses its context: discoveries pile up without use, and inventions are built without checking what is already known. Every record here specifies one step of the unified workflow completely enough that a researcher could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Opportunity Scanning as one working part of the Universal Discovery and Invention Center. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers the unified workflow: opportunity scanning, problem framing, prior-knowledge search, hypothesis formation, experiment design, prototype invention, novelty checking, feasibility assessment, invention records, discovery logs, cross-domain transfer, serendipity capture, research planning, evidence collection, peer review inside the system, invention disclosure, iteration cycles, failure records, breakthrough criteria, technology readiness, invention-to-construction handover, discovery metrics, the inventor's notebook, and the discovery and invention lifecycle. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the discoveries and inventions themselves, which belong to their own subject databases, and the construction of finished inventions, which belongs to the creation and construction database. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements, as design requirements: Every workflow step in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must name its inputs, including the prior knowledge that must be searched before new work begins. Second, it must name its outputs, including the record that proves the step happened. Third, it must be reproducible: another researcher following the same steps with the same inputs must reach the same result. Fourth, it must record failures as carefully as successes, because a failure record prevents the next researcher from repeating it. Fifth, it must check novelty against what already exists, so nothing is claimed as new that is not. Sixth, it must state its evidence plainly, with sources and dates. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: The workflow connects through four interfaces. The search interface queries the live research-discovery and systems-invention databases before new work begins, so prior knowledge is found first. The notebook interface records every observation, hypothesis, and test in dated order, so the path of the work can be replayed. The review interface sends each result to peer review inside the system, with the reviewers, the verdict, and the date recorded. The handover interface passes a tested invention to the creation and construction database with its specification, its evidence, and its open questions. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every handoff is a written record, so nothing is passed along by word of mouth. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Opportunity Scanning, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Opportunity Scanning inside the Universal Discovery and Invention Center: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live research-discovery database, with 10,000 records, and the live systems-invention database, with 10,275 records, which cover discovery and invention as separate single subjects. Neither unifies the two into one workflow under the paste's section 7 lifecycle, which is the gap this database fills. It also links the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, and the live simulation databases, which test inventions once they exist.

Verification path: Re-read the catalog item #67 and the cross-check row for it; confirm the slug and prefix are the pair's own; run this record's demo and confirm Deterministic: MATCH; confirm the design-study label, the maturity label, and the implementation status appear in the record; confirm the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions appear in plain words; confirm every cited source returns HTTP 200.

Its outputs are complete deliverables, not notes: a written specification, a checked result, and a record that the next step can read and test without asking the author what was meant. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers the unified workflow: opportunity scanning, problem framing, prior-knowledge search, hypothesis formation, experiment design, prototype invention, novelty checking, feasibility assessment, invention records, discovery logs, cross-domain transfer, serendipity capture, research planning, evidence collection, peer review inside the system, invention disclosure, iteration cycles, failure records, breakthrough criteria, technology readiness, invention-to-construction handover, discovery metrics, the inventor's notebook, and the discovery and invention lifecycle. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the discoveries and inventions themselves, which belong to their own subject databases, and the construction of finished inventions, which belongs to the creation and construction database. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements, as design requirements: Every workflow step in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must name its inputs, including the prior knowledge that must be searched before new work begins. Second, it must name its outputs, including the record that proves the step happened. Third, it must be reproducible: another researcher following the same steps with the same inputs must reach the same result. Fourth, it must record failures as carefully as successes, because a failure record prevents the next researcher from repeating it. Fifth, it must check novelty against what already exists, so nothing is claimed as new that is not. Sixth, it must state its evidence plainly, with sources and dates. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: The workflow connects through four interfaces. The search interface queries the live research-discovery and systems-invention databases before new work begins, so prior knowledge is found first. The notebook interface records every observation, hypothesis, and test in dated order, so the path of the work can be replayed. The review interface sends each result to peer review inside the system, with the reviewers, the verdict, and the date recorded. The handover interface passes a tested invention to the creation and construction database with its specification, its evidence, and its open questions. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every handoff is a written record, so nothing is passed along by word of mouth. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Problem Framing, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Problem Framing inside the Universal Discovery and Invention Center: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live research-discovery database, with 10,000 records, and the live systems-invention database, with 10,275 records, which cover discovery and invention as separate single subjects. Neither unifies the two into one workflow under the paste's section 7 lifecycle, which is the gap this database fills. It also links the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, and the live simulation databases, which test inventions once they exist.

Signature Prior-Knowledge Search: Universal Discovery and Invention

JAH-UDIS-000003 · Prior-Knowledge Search

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Prior-Knowledge Search inside the Universal Discovery and Invention Center. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record discovery and invention as one connected workflow, from the first sight of an opportunity to a tested invention ready for construction. Discovery is finding what is true and not yet known; invention is making what is useful and does not yet exist; in practice they feed each other, because a discovery suggests an invention and an invention reveals new things to discover. Kept apart, each loses its context: discoveries pile up without use, and inventions are built without checking what is already known. Every record here specifies one step of the unified workflow completely enough that a researcher could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Prior-Knowledge Search as one working part of the Universal Discovery and Invention Center. Its working angle is checks. Its checks are run by execution, not by reading: the demo recomputes the record's figures twice and compares digests, the program reruns the same computation, and any mismatch stops the step. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: The workflow connects through four interfaces. The search interface queries the live research-discovery and systems-invention databases before new work begins, so prior knowledge is found first. The notebook interface records every observation, hypothesis, and test in dated order, so the path of the work can be replayed. The review interface sends each result to peer review inside the system, with the reviewers, the verdict, and the date recorded. The handover interface passes a tested invention to the creation and construction database with its specification, its evidence, and its open questions. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every handoff is a written record, so nothing is passed along by word of mouth. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Prototype Invention, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Prototype Invention inside the Universal Discovery and Invention Center: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live research-discovery database, with 10,000 records, and the live systems-invention database, with 10,275 records, which cover discovery and invention as separate single subjects. Neither unifies the two into one workflow under the paste's section 7 lifecycle, which is the gap this database fills. It also links the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, and the live simulation databases, which test inventions once they exist.

Signature Novelty Checking: Universal Discovery and Invention

JAH-UDIS-000007 · Novelty Checking

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Novelty Checking inside the Universal Discovery and Invention Center. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record discovery and invention as one connected workflow, from the first sight of an opportunity to a tested invention ready for construction. Discovery is finding what is true and not yet known; invention is making what is useful and does not yet exist; in practice they feed each other, because a discovery suggests an invention and an invention reveals new things to discover. Kept apart, each loses its context: discoveries pile up without use, and inventions are built without checking what is already known. Every record here specifies one step of the unified workflow completely enough that a researcher could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Novelty Checking as one working part of the Universal Discovery and Invention Center. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers the unified workflow: opportunity scanning, problem framing, prior-knowledge search, hypothesis formation, experiment design, prototype invention, novelty checking, feasibility assessment, invention records, discovery logs, cross-domain transfer, serendipity capture, research planning, evidence collection, peer review inside the system, invention disclosure, iteration cycles, failure records, breakthrough criteria, technology readiness, invention-to-construction handover, discovery metrics, the inventor's notebook, and the discovery and invention lifecycle. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the discoveries and inventions themselves, which belong to their own subject databases, and the construction of finished inventions, which belongs to the creation and construction database. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements, as design requirements: Every workflow step in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must name its inputs, including the prior knowledge that must be searched before new work begins. Second, it must name its outputs, including the record that proves the step happened. Third, it must be reproducible: another researcher following the same steps with the same inputs must reach the same result. Fourth, it must record failures as carefully as successes, because a failure record prevents the next researcher from repeating it. Fifth, it must check novelty against what already exists, so nothing is claimed as new that is not. Sixth, it must state its evidence plainly, with sources and dates. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: The workflow connects through four interfaces. The search interface queries the live research-discovery and systems-invention databases before new work begins, so prior knowledge is found first. The notebook interface records every observation, hypothesis, and test in dated order, so the path of the work can be replayed. The review interface sends each result to peer review inside the system, with the reviewers, the verdict, and the date recorded. The handover interface passes a tested invention to the creation and construction database with its specification, its evidence, and its open questions. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every handoff is a written record, so nothing is passed along by word of mouth. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Discovery Logs, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Discovery Logs inside the Universal Discovery and Invention Center: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live research-discovery database, with 10,000 records, and the live systems-invention database, with 10,275 records, which cover discovery and invention as separate single subjects. Neither unifies the two into one workflow under the paste's section 7 lifecycle, which is the gap this database fills. It also links the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, and the live simulation databases, which test inventions once they exist.

Signature Cross-Domain Transfer: Universal Discovery and Invention

JAH-UDIS-000011 · Cross-Domain Transfer

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Cross-Domain Transfer inside the Universal Discovery and Invention Center. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record discovery and invention as one connected workflow, from the first sight of an opportunity to a tested invention ready for construction. Discovery is finding what is true and not yet known; invention is making what is useful and does not yet exist; in practice they feed each other, because a discovery suggests an invention and an invention reveals new things to discover. Kept apart, each loses its context: discoveries pile up without use, and inventions are built without checking what is already known. Every record here specifies one step of the unified workflow completely enough that a researcher could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Cross-Domain Transfer as one working part of the Universal Discovery and Invention Center. Its working angle is handover. Its handover is a named handoff: the receiving step gets the specification, the evidence, and the open questions in writing, and the handover is only complete when the receiver can run the checks. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers the unified workflow: opportunity scanning, problem framing, prior-knowledge search, hypothesis formation, experiment design, prototype invention, novelty checking, feasibility assessment, invention records, discovery logs, cross-domain transfer, serendipity capture, research planning, evidence collection, peer review inside the system, invention disclosure, iteration cycles, failure records, breakthrough criteria, technology readiness, invention-to-construction handover, discovery metrics, the inventor's notebook, and the discovery and invention lifecycle. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the discoveries and inventions themselves, which belong to their own subject databases, and the construction of finished inventions, which belongs to the creation and construction database. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements, as design requirements: Every workflow step in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must name its inputs, including the prior knowledge that must be searched before new work begins. Second, it must name its outputs, including the record that proves the step happened. Third, it must be reproducible: another researcher following the same steps with the same inputs must reach the same result. Fourth, it must record failures as carefully as successes, because a failure record prevents the next researcher from repeating it. Fifth, it must check novelty against what already exists, so nothing is claimed as new that is not. Sixth, it must state its evidence plainly, with sources and dates. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: The workflow connects through four interfaces. The search interface queries the live research-discovery and systems-invention databases before new work begins, so prior knowledge is found first. The notebook interface records every observation, hypothesis, and test in dated order, so the path of the work can be replayed. The review interface sends each result to peer review inside the system, with the reviewers, the verdict, and the date recorded. The handover interface passes a tested invention to the creation and construction database with its specification, its evidence, and its open questions. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every handoff is a written record, so nothing is passed along by word of mouth. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Cross-Domain Transfer, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: theoretical, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Evidence Collection inside the Universal Discovery and Invention Center: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live research-discovery database, with 10,000 records, and the live systems-invention database, with 10,275 records, which cover discovery and invention as separate single subjects. Neither unifies the two into one workflow under the paste's section 7 lifecycle, which is the gap this database fills. It also links the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, and the live simulation databases, which test inventions once they exist.

Signature Peer Review Inside the System: Universal Discovery and Invention

JAH-UDIS-000015 · Peer Review Inside the System

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Peer Review Inside the System inside the Universal Discovery and Invention Center. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record discovery and invention as one connected workflow, from the first sight of an opportunity to a tested invention ready for construction. Discovery is finding what is true and not yet known; invention is making what is useful and does not yet exist; in practice they feed each other, because a discovery suggests an invention and an invention reveals new things to discover. Kept apart, each loses its context: discoveries pile up without use, and inventions are built without checking what is already known. Every record here specifies one step of the unified workflow completely enough that a researcher could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Peer Review Inside the System as one working part of the Universal Discovery and Invention Center. Its working angle is checks. Its checks are run by execution, not by reading: the demo recomputes the record's figures twice and compares digests, the program reruns the same computation, and any mismatch stops the step. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers the unified workflow: opportunity scanning, problem framing, prior-knowledge search, hypothesis formation, experiment design, prototype invention, novelty checking, feasibility assessment, invention records, discovery logs, cross-domain transfer, serendipity capture, research planning, evidence collection, peer review inside the system, invention disclosure, iteration cycles, failure records, breakthrough criteria, technology readiness, invention-to-construction handover, discovery metrics, the inventor's notebook, and the discovery and invention lifecycle. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the discoveries and inventions themselves, which belong to their own subject databases, and the construction of finished inventions, which belongs to the creation and construction database. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements, as design requirements: Every workflow step in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must name its inputs, including the prior knowledge that must be searched before new work begins. Second, it must name its outputs, including the record that proves the step happened. Third, it must be reproducible: another researcher following the same steps with the same inputs must reach the same result. Fourth, it must record failures as carefully as successes, because a failure record prevents the next researcher from repeating it. Fifth, it must check novelty against what already exists, so nothing is claimed as new that is not. Sixth, it must state its evidence plainly, with sources and dates. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: The workflow connects through four interfaces. The search interface queries the live research-discovery and systems-invention databases before new work begins, so prior knowledge is found first. The notebook interface records every observation, hypothesis, and test in dated order, so the path of the work can be replayed. The review interface sends each result to peer review inside the system, with the reviewers, the verdict, and the date recorded. The handover interface passes a tested invention to the creation and construction database with its specification, its evidence, and its open questions. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every handoff is a written record, so nothing is passed along by word of mouth. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Peer Review Inside the System, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: generated, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Peer Review Inside the System inside the Universal Discovery and Invention Center: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. Neither unifies the two into one workflow under the paste's section 7 lifecycle, which is the gap this database fills. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Failure Records inside the Universal Discovery and Invention Center: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live research-discovery database, with 10,000 records, and the live systems-invention database, with 10,275 records, which cover discovery and invention as separate single subjects. Neither unifies the two into one workflow under the paste's section 7 lifecycle, which is the gap this database fills. It also links the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, and the live simulation databases, which test inventions once they exist.

Signature Breakthrough Criteria: Universal Discovery and Invention

JAH-UDIS-000019 · Breakthrough Criteria

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Breakthrough Criteria inside the Universal Discovery and Invention Center. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record discovery and invention as one connected workflow, from the first sight of an opportunity to a tested invention ready for construction. Discovery is finding what is true and not yet known; invention is making what is useful and does not yet exist; in practice they feed each other, because a discovery suggests an invention and an invention reveals new things to discover. Kept apart, each loses its context: discoveries pile up without use, and inventions are built without checking what is already known. Every record here specifies one step of the unified workflow completely enough that a researcher could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Breakthrough Criteria as one working part of the Universal Discovery and Invention Center. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers the unified workflow: opportunity scanning, problem framing, prior-knowledge search, hypothesis formation, experiment design, prototype invention, novelty checking, feasibility assessment, invention records, discovery logs, cross-domain transfer, serendipity capture, research planning, evidence collection, peer review inside the system, invention disclosure, iteration cycles, failure records, breakthrough criteria, technology readiness, invention-to-construction handover, discovery metrics, the inventor's notebook, and the discovery and invention lifecycle. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the discoveries and inventions themselves, which belong to their own subject databases, and the construction of finished inventions, which belongs to the creation and construction database. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements, as design requirements: Every workflow step in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must name its inputs, including the prior knowledge that must be searched before new work begins. Second, it must name its outputs, including the record that proves the step happened. Third, it must be reproducible: another researcher following the same steps with the same inputs must reach the same result. Fourth, it must record failures as carefully as successes, because a failure record prevents the next researcher from repeating it. Fifth, it must check novelty against what already exists, so nothing is claimed as new that is not. Sixth, it must state its evidence plainly, with sources and dates. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: The workflow connects through four interfaces. The search interface queries the live research-discovery and systems-invention databases before new work begins, so prior knowledge is found first. The notebook interface records every observation, hypothesis, and test in dated order, so the path of the work can be replayed. The review interface sends each result to peer review inside the system, with the reviewers, the verdict, and the date recorded. The handover interface passes a tested invention to the creation and construction database with its specification, its evidence, and its open questions. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every handoff is a written record, so nothing is passed along by word of mouth. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Breakthrough Criteria, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Breakthrough Criteria inside the Universal Discovery and Invention Center: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live research-discovery database, with 10,000 records, and the live systems-invention database, with 10,275 records, which cover discovery and invention as separate single subjects. Neither unifies the two into one workflow under the paste's section 7 lifecycle, which is the gap this database fills. It also links the Foundation program's staged universal-knowledge pair and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, and the live simulation databases, which test inventions once they exist.

Verification path: Re-read the catalog item #67 and the cross-check row for it; confirm the slug and prefix are the pair's own; run this record's demo and confirm Deterministic: MATCH; confirm the design-study label, the maturity label, and the implementation status appear in the record; confirm the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions appear in plain words; confirm every cited source returns HTTP 200.
